Chapter 0238: Organelles and Intracellular Systems

414_Structures | 1001 Theorems and Proofs
« Previous Index Next »

Table of Contents

Theorems 1-1001 on Organelles and Intracellular Systems in 414_Structures.

Theorem 0001 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 1. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0002 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 2. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0003 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 3. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0041 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 41. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0042 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 42. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0080 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 80. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0081 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 81. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0120 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 120. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0121 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 121. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0159 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 159. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0160 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 160. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 198. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0199 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 199. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0238 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 238. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0239 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 239. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 277. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0317 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 317. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0318 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 318. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0356 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 356. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0357 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 357. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0396 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 396. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0397 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 397. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0435 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 435. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0436 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 436. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 474. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0475 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 475. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0514 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 514. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0515 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 515. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 553. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Theorem 554. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0593 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 593. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0594 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 594. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

Theorem 0632 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 632. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0633 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 633. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 671. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. Theorem 672. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Laplacian $\Delta u = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x_i^2}$ acting on $u \in C^2(\Omega) \cap C(\overline{\Omega})$ satisfies the maximum principle: if $\Delta u \geq 0$ in $\Omega$ (subharmonic), then $\max_{\overline{\Omega}} u = \max_{\partial \Omega} u$. For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0711 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 711. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0712 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 712. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0750 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 750. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0751 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 751. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0789 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 789. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0790 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 790. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0828 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 828. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0829 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 829. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0867 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 867. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0868 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 868. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0906 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 906. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0907 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 907. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0945 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 945. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0946 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 946. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.

For the Dirichlet problem $-\Delta u = f$ in $\Omega$, $u = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, existence follows from Lax-Milgram on $H^1_0(\Omega)$: $a(u,v) = \int_\Omega \nabla u \cdot \nabla v\,dx$ is coercive on $H^1_0$.

Theorem 0984 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 984. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. Consider the operator $T: L^2(\Omega) \to L^2(\Omega)$ defined by $(Tf)(x) = \int_\Omega K(x,y)f(y)\,dy$ where the kernel satisfies $|K(x,y)| \leq M \cdot e^{-\alpha|x-y|}$ for some $M > 0$, $\alpha > 0$. The Schur test gives $\|T\| \leq M \cdot \sup_x \int_\Omega e^{-\alpha|x-y|}\,dy \leq 2M/\alpha$. Since $T$ is bounded, it extends to a continuous linear operator by the closed graph theorem.

Theorem 0985 Organelles and Intracellular Systems

Theorem 985. In the context of organelles, let the relevant algebraic structure be defined on a suitable domain. Then the fundamental relationship holds under the stated regularity conditions.

Proof. The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ of the Lie group $G$ carries the bracket operation $[X, Y]$ defined via the adjoint representation. The exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ is a local diffeomorphism near the identity. For matrix Lie groups, $\exp(X) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{X^k}{k!}$. Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff gives $\exp(X)\exp(Y) = \exp(X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X,Y] + \cdots)$, so the group law near the identity is determined entirely by the Lie bracket.
